Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.

And then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.

It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.

But then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.

But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?

And I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.

It’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.

Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.

Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.

A lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.

It’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating, once you've stopped and reflected on what happened?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. So afterward it feels like I’ve let myself down in this private, embarrassing way. Like, “How can I be that kind of person at work and then come home and do this?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.

I’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.

It’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.

Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.

Work can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.

So… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating, once you've stopped and reflected on what happened?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. So afterward it feels like I’ve let myself down in this private, embarrassing way. Like, “How can I be that kind of person at work and then come home and do this?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.\n\nIt’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”

I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.

So I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.

I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.

It’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.

So… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.

I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.\n\nIt’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Lately… I’d say it’s been at least once a week, and more often it’s like two or three nights in a week. Not every single night, but enough that it feels like a pattern now.

It usually seems to be after a late shift or after one of those rough days on the line where I get home and I’m tired and kind of worn down. Then I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have something quick, and it turns into… more than that. And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”

For the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating, once you've stopped and reflected on what happened?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. So afterward it feels like I’ve let myself down in this private, embarrassing way. Like, “How can I be that kind of person at work and then come home and do this?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.\n\nIt’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… I’d say it’s been at least once a week, and more often it’s like two or three nights in a week. Not every single night, but enough that it feels like a pattern now.\n\nIt usually seems to be after a late shift or after one of those rough days on the line where I get home and I’m tired and kind of worn down. Then I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have something quick, and it turns into… more than that. And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nFor the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nFor the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there are any specific emotions that tend to trigger these episodes of eating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it seems like there should be some obvious feeling right before it happens, but it’s not always that clear to me.

Sometimes I think it’s just being worn out, like mentally tired more than anything. But whether that’s an emotion or just being exhausted, I don’t know. I don’t always sit there and think, “I’m sad,” or “I’m angry,” and then go eat. It’s more like I get home and I’m kind of… emptied out, I guess, and then I don’t really want to deal with anything or think too much.

There might be stress in there, or frustration, but I don’t know if I’ve tracked it closely enough to say there’s one specific emotion that sets it off. I wish I had a cleaner answer, but most of the time I only realize afterward that maybe I was feeling something and I just wasn’t paying attention to it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating, once you've stopped and reflected on what happened?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. So afterward it feels like I’ve let myself down in this private, embarrassing way. Like, “How can I be that kind of person at work and then come home and do this?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.\n\nIt’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… I’d say it’s been at least once a week, and more often it’s like two or three nights in a week. Not every single night, but enough that it feels like a pattern now.\n\nIt usually seems to be after a late shift or after one of those rough days on the line where I get home and I’m tired and kind of worn down. Then I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have something quick, and it turns into… more than that. And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nFor the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any specific emotions that tend to trigger these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it seems like there should be some obvious feeling right before it happens, but it’s not always that clear to me.\n\nSometimes I think it’s just being worn out, like mentally tired more than anything. But whether that’s an emotion or just being exhausted, I don’t know. I don’t always sit there and think, “I’m sad,” or “I’m angry,” and then go eat. It’s more like I get home and I’m kind of… emptied out, I guess, and then I don’t really want to deal with anything or think too much.\n\nThere might be stress in there, or frustration, but I don’t know if I’ve tracked it closely enough to say there’s one specific emotion that sets it off. I wish I had a cleaner answer, but most of the time I only realize afterward that maybe I was feeling something and I just wasn’t paying attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… I’d say it’s been at least once a week, and more often it’s like two or three nights in a week. Not every single night, but enough that it feels like a pattern now.\n\nIt usually seems to be after a late shift or after one of those rough days on the line where I get home and I’m tired and kind of worn down. Then I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have something quick, and it turns into… more than that. And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nFor the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any specific emotions that tend to trigger these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it seems like there should be some obvious feeling right before it happens, but it’s not always that clear to me.\n\nSometimes I think it’s just being worn out, like mentally tired more than anything. But whether that’s an emotion or just being exhausted, I don’t know. I don’t always sit there and think, “I’m sad,” or “I’m angry,” and then go eat. It’s more like I get home and I’m kind of… emptied out, I guess, and then I don’t really want to deal with anything or think too much.\n\nThere might be stress in there, or frustration, but I don’t know if I’ve tracked it closely enough to say there’s one specific emotion that sets it off. I wish I had a cleaner answer, but most of the time I only realize afterward that maybe I was feeling something and I just wasn’t paying attention to it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel full or uncomfortable during these episodes of eating, or does that not really register until afterward?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It does register, yeah… not always right at the beginning, but by the time I’m near the end, it’s pretty hard to miss. I’ll be sitting there and my stomach feels tight, like pressing against my work pants, especially if I haven’t even changed out of them yet. That’s actually one of the things that makes me realize how far it went.

It’s not just “I’m full” like after a regular meal. It’s more like I’m leaned back in the chair or on the couch because sitting normally feels uncomfortable, and I don’t really want to move for a while. Like getting up to throw things away or even just standing feels like too much because my stomach feels stretched and heavy.

So I guess it starts to register during it, but I don’t always stop when I first notice it. By the time I do stop, I’m definitely uncomfortable.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating after work, especially after late shifts. I’m a production worker, so when I get off late I’m tired and keyed up at the same time, and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick. But then I’ll get takeout and it’s not just a normal meal—like, I’ll end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, the whole thing.\n\nAnd then the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed is I’ll still go home afterward and… keep eating. Like I’ll go into the garage where we keep extra leftovers sometimes, and I’ll clean those out too, almost like it’s a second supper. I know that sounds like a lot, and it is. It feels like once I start, I just keep going past the point where it makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when this happens, or does it feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: It feels like I’m not in control, honestly. Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. It’s like the thinking part of me is there, but it’s not the part driving. I don’t know if that makes sense. I’ll have this whole argument in my head while I’m still eating, and even though I know I should stop, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating, once you've stopped and reflected on what happened?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… pretty awful, honestly. Not just physically, but like this heavy shame kind of settles in once I’ve stopped and I’m looking at the wrappers or the containers or whatever’s left. I get this feeling like, what is wrong with me, why did I do that again?\n\nAnd I think part of why it hits me so hard is I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined person. I mean, I show up for work, I do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. I’ve tried to be dependable my whole life. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. So afterward it feels like I’ve let myself down in this private, embarrassing way. Like, “How can I be that kind of person at work and then come home and do this?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. Like at dinner with my wife, I might act like I’m not that hungry, or I’ll eat a normal-looking amount, maybe even say I’m full. And then later, if she’s watching TV or on the phone or busy with something, I’ll find a reason to go outside. Sometimes I’ll go out to my truck or out to the workshop, and that’s where I’ll eat more.\n\nIt’s not because I want some peaceful snack or something. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really paid close attention to whether I’m feeling guilty right then or whether that comes mostly after.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… I’d say it’s been at least once a week, and more often it’s like two or three nights in a week. Not every single night, but enough that it feels like a pattern now.\n\nIt usually seems to be after a late shift or after one of those rough days on the line where I get home and I’m tired and kind of worn down. Then I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have something quick, and it turns into… more than that. And then afterward I’m thinking, “Okay, that was one of those nights again.”\n\nFor the past few months, that’s probably the best way to put it: usually at least one night a week, sometimes two or three.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any specific emotions that tend to trigger these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it seems like there should be some obvious feeling right before it happens, but it’s not always that clear to me.\n\nSometimes I think it’s just being worn out, like mentally tired more than anything. But whether that’s an emotion or just being exhausted, I don’t know. I don’t always sit there and think, “I’m sad,” or “I’m angry,” and then go eat. It’s more like I get home and I’m kind of… emptied out, I guess, and then I don’t really want to deal with anything or think too much.\n\nThere might be stress in there, or frustration, but I don’t know if I’ve tracked it closely enough to say there’s one specific emotion that sets it off. I wish I had a cleaner answer, but most of the time I only realize afterward that maybe I was feeling something and I just wasn’t paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel full or uncomfortable during these episodes of eating, or does that not really register until afterward?\n\nPatient: It does register, yeah… not always right at the beginning, but by the time I’m near the end, it’s pretty hard to miss. I’ll be sitting there and my stomach feels tight, like pressing against my work pants, especially if I haven’t even changed out of them yet. By the time I do stop, I’m definitely uncomfortable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I definitely know I feel bad afterward, like once I stop and look at what I ate, that’s when it really hits me. But while I’m actually eating… I don’t know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. Then afterward it’s harder to avoid, because the wrappers or containers are there, or I just feel physically uncomfortable, and then I’m more self-critical.\n\nSo… during it, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure. Yeah… that’s probably the main thing that bothers me about it, honestly. It isn’t just that I decide to eat too much and then I’m careless about it. It feels more like once I start, something kind of flips over in my head.

I’ll be aware of it while it’s happening, too. Like I’ll literally be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag. Put it away. Go inside. You’ve had enough.” And then my hand is already reaching back in before I’ve even finished that thought. Or I’ll say, “Okay, this is the last one,” and then a minute later I’m doing it again.

That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ashamed afterward, because it’s not like I don’t know. I do know. I’ll want to stop, or at least part of me wants to stop, but in that moment it feels like the wanting doesn’t have much pull. My body just keeps going along with it, like I’m on autopilot. Then when it’s over, I’m sitting there thinking, “Why didn’t you just stop when you knew you should?”

Like once I start, something just kind of flips over in me. I can be standing there with the bag open or the container in my hands, and part of me is completely aware of what I’m doing. I’ll even be saying to myself, Roy, shut the bag, put it down, go inside, you’ve had enough.\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back in. So when I’m sneaking food or eating out in the garage like I don’t want anyone to see, it makes me feel… kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’m letting myself down in this really private, embarrassing way.\n\nIt’s not just “I ate too much.” It feels like I went against who I’m supposed to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during the eating episode itself, or is the guilt something that you feel afterward?\n\nPatient: Mostly afterward, I think. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. I wait until I’m alone, or I make it look like I’m doing something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed, anxious, or upset, or is it mostly just after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely enough to tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating during these episodes, not just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. Like getting up to throw things away or even just standing feels like too much because my stomach feels stretched and heavy.\n\nSo I guess it starts to register during it, but I don’t always stop when I first notice it. By the time I do stop, I’m definitely uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the main thing that bothers me about it, honestly. I mean, during it there might be a little voice in my head saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt doesn’t land until I’ve stopped.\n\nThat’s when I look around and see what I ate, or I realize I was kind of sneaking around with it, and I just feel ashamed. Almost disgusted with myself, honestly. Because in the rest of my life I’ve always tried to be disciplined and dependable—go to work, do my job, do what I’m supposed to do. I think that’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I look back on it, because it’s not even like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nA lot of times I’m out in my truck or in the workshop, and I’m eating so fast it’s almost like I’m trying to get it over with before somebody notices. Like I have to get it down quick, get rid of the evidence, whatever. And half the time I barely even taste what I’m eating. I’ll realize afterward that I went through a whole bag or container and I can’t even really say I enjoyed it, not in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just shoveling it in. Like my hands are moving faster than my brain is, if that makes sense. And there’s this rushed feeling to it, like I’m sneaking something and I need to hurry up before someone catches me, even if nobody’s actually coming.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from family members or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that, but yes.\n\nI’ll kind of manage it so it doesn’t look like anything is going on. It’s because I’d be really embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away. I don’t want her standing there watching me eat like that, because I know it would look… I don’t know, out of control. So I hide it. I mean, after work is when it seems to happen most, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m stressed or because that’s just when I’m finally alone and the day is over.\n\nWork can be tiring, and there are days where I come home wound up or irritated, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, every time I’m anxious, I eat like that.” Sometimes it feels more like a habit or like once I get in that mode, it just kind of starts. Other times I think maybe I am trying to take the edge off without saying that to myself.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’ve noticed it’s often after work, but I don’t know for sure if stress or being upset is the main trigger. I haven’t really paid attention in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say for sure it’s because of those episodes. I mean… my weight has gone up and down some over the years, like I think most people’s does, especially getting older and with work and all that. But I haven’t kept close track of it in a way where I could point to it and say, “That happened because I was eating like this.”\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. Sometimes I’ll notice my pants feel tighter, or I’ll feel heavier, or I’ll avoid the scale because I don’t really want the number in my head. But as far as a significant change, I’m not sure. I haven’t sat down and connected the dots like that.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… maybe, but I don’t really know. Sometimes I think there’s a little voice in the back of my head like, “What are you doing?” but I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly in the moment.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying not to think too much while it’s happening. Like if I really let myself think about it, I’d probably stop or at least feel worse, so I kind of push that part away. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that directly, no. I mean, my weight has gone up over time, but if you’re asking whether after one of those nights I step on the scale and it’s suddenly jumped in some major way, I can’t really say that I’ve tracked it like that.

I don’t weigh myself every day, partly because I don’t like seeing it, if I’m being honest. So I might avoid the scale for a while, and then when I do check, I’ll think, “Well, that’s higher than I wanted,” but I can’t tie it to one specific episode or even one specific week. It feels more gradual than that in my mind.

Afterward I can feel heavier, if that makes sense — bloated, uncomfortable, like my clothes are tighter — but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain or just how I feel physically after eating that much. So… significant increase over a short period? I’m not sure. I haven’t really measured it closely enough to know.
